On  the  thirteenth  day  of  September  1815,, 
we  Aaron  Smith,  Xi rah  B.  VVheelan.  James 
M.  Von  Nostrand  and  Edward  Whitcomb,

n Prophet  and'  Seer  of  God.  He  procee*

ancient  people  was  buried  in  a hill  south  of 
White  river  bridge  near  the  east  line  of 
Walworth  Count y , And  leading'  us  to  on 
oak  tree  about  one  foot  in  diame'er,  tgjd

the  ground  that  we  should  know  we  were 
not  imposed  upon,  and  that  it  had  not  been 
burred  there  since  the  tree  grew.  The  tree 
was  surrounded  by  o sward  of  deeply  root- 
ed grass  such  as  is  usually  found  in  the 
openings,  and.  upon  the  most  critical  ex- 

oation  that  ithadever  beencu  i through

The  illustration  is  in  every  sense  bad.  By

On  the* death  of  Joseph,  the  twelve  who

BROTHER  THIRTEEN. 

Who  is  brother  thirteen  ?,^Amasa  Ly- 
man. Whydo  you  call  himoy  that  game? 
Because  ke  is  the  thirteenth  member  of  tho 
Tibelve*  Whai  ? are  there  thirteen  of  the 
Twelve  1 There-  were  twelve  withouthiin. 
Well,  is  he  one  of  them  ? - That  is  rather 
uncertain. 

Amasa  Lyman  by  some  means  grew  to 
be  considered  a counsellor  to  Joseph.  On 
the  death  of  Joseph,  the  Twelve  were  wil- 
ling to  let  Rigdon  and  Lyman  set  with  them 
but  hoi  above  them.  Rigdon  would  not  do 
it,  but  Lyman  did.  At  the  October  confer- 
ence 1841,  votes  were  taken  on  all  the 
Twelve  separately  by  name  to  sustain* them, 
severally  as  members  of  the  Twelve,  ando 
they  werecartied  unanimously.  Times  and 
S.  p 6&2.  ‘ ' ’ \ ' 

“ Moved  by  Elder  H.  C.  'ffimball,  that 
Elder  Amasa  Lyman  stand  in  Ais  lot . The 
motion  was  seconded. ” 

“ President  Young  said  by  way  of  ex- 
planation, that  Elder  Amasa  Lyman  is  ones 
of  Twelve  just  in  the  same  relationship  as 
he  sustained  to  the  first  Presidency . He  ia 
one  in  our  midst,  and  a counsellor  with  us.” 

“The,  motion  was  then  presented  and  car- 
ried unanimously.” 

Since  that  time  he  has  there  been  recog- 
nized as  one  of  the  Twelve,  though  there 
were  twelve  without  him.  Some  .suppose 
he  stands  in  Lyman  Wright’s  place,  but  he 
was  unanimously  sustained  at  the  same  con- 
ference, and  has  not  been  rejected  since. 
Others  think,  he  stands  in  Wm.  Srmth’ti 
place.  But  he  was  also  unaimously  sus- 
tained at  the  same  conference,  and  it  was 
not  till  about  the  time  of  the  October  con- 
ference 18*5  that  they  prepared  to  drop 
him. 

By  the  way  who  ordained  Amasa  Lyman, 
and  to  what  office!  I can  find  no  evidence 
that  he  was  ever  ordained  a counsellor.  But 
suppose  he  was;  ordaining  biro  to  one  priest- 
hood does  not  give  him  another.  Who  or* 
dained  him  an  Apostle ? No  one.  The 
conference  vgt^d  to  sustain  him  in  his  lot, 
which  was  not  an  Apostlcship.  Certainly 
a roost  singular,  mode  of  obtaining  the 
Apostlcship,  or  any  otbej^prmslhood  in  thin 

Church. £ ;

At  the  October  conference  1841,  a reso-

They  have  assumed  that  there  is  no  suc- 
cession in  the  Priesthood  for  the  purpose  of 
getting  rid  of  the  necessity  of  a successor 
to  Joseph,  and  coming  to  an  apostle  who  is 
a successor  to  another  who  fell  a martyr  to 
the  faith,  ihcy  attempt  tu  remo ve  the  effect

ly a temporary  substitute.  But  in  so  doing 
they  have  changed,  not  removed,  the  diffi- 
culty. If  David  Patten, .as  a martyred  apos- 
tle has  a successor  to  finish  his  work  on 
earth,  while  he  is  ministering  to  the  world 
of  Spirits,  so  should  Joseph,  ss  a martyred 
President,  have  a successor  to  finish  bis 
work  on  earth,  while  ho  is  ministering  to 
the  world  of  spirits.  And  the  conference 
who  were  so  very  careful  to  prevent  Pitten’s 
successor  from  taking  his  crown,  should 
have  also  voted,  if  they  would  be  consistent 
with  themselves,  “That  Elder  James  J. 
Strang  be  sustained  in  bis  office,  to  fill  the 
place  of  Elder  Joseph  Smith,  (tm  ft yred)buf 
not  to  take  his  crown.”

THE  FUTURE. 


We  have  spoken  of  the  past  and  talked 
of  its  errors  till  the  subject  is  sickening. 
This  was  not  because  we  delighted  in  ex- 
posing our  brethien’s  faults,- but  because  it 
was  the  only  way  in  which'-  the  unwary 
could  be  saved  from  destruction.  Shall 
there  be  evil  in  the  city,  and  § ha  I L-.no  t . the 
watchman  cry  aloud? 

But  we  turn  to  the  future,  with  its  toils 
and  its  hopes.  The  first  thing  to  be  done 
is  to' renovate  the  Church.  Not  to  build  up 
a new  Church,  but  to  upbuild'the  old  ac- 
cording to  the  pattern.  There  must  be  a

his  defence  be  heard.  5th,  that  on  such

of  excommunication  be  passed  upon. him. 

If  any  one  of  these  facts  be  lacking  the 
individual  concerned  is  not  cut  off  from  the 
church  nor  his  standing  therein  in  any 
way  affected.  The  vote  of  excommunica- 
tion in  such  case  would  be  utterly  void,  and 
the  church  have  nothing  to  do  but  to  disre- 
gard it.  It  is  not  even  necessary  to  ap- 
peal from  it,  tor  the  judgment  being  a mere 
nullity  there  is  nothing  to  appeal  from.

fatir,  id  imtirely  self-educated  and  a man 
of  extensive  and  general  reading.  He  is  no  w 
Ongaged  in  connection  with  several  leading 
' citizens,  in  devising  an  enlarged  and  liberal 
system  of  com.  schools  for  Wisconsin;  is  a 
warm  advocate  of  temperance,  and  more  or 
less  connected  with  most  of  the  benevolent 
enterprises  of  the  s^e.  In  public  speaking, 
his  enunciation  is  tolerably  distinct,  very 
vapid  and  somewhat  too  loud.  He  is  a close, 
debater,  generally  .mild  in  criticism,  but  in 
invective  comes  down  like  an  avalanche. — 
Both  his  views  and  his  plans  are  very  com- 
prehensive and  look  forward  to  future-gen- 
erations as  much  as  to  the  present. 

He  deprecates- both  the  military  and  the  , 
mob  spirit ; looks  upon  the  organization  of 
military  bands  in  the  church  as  uncalled  for, 
and  a most  fruitful  source  of  opposition  and 
jealousy,  and  goes  very  near  as  far  as  the 
Quakers  for  non-resistance ; looking  to 
peaceful  avocations  as  a better  security 
against  molestation,  than  any  armed  defence 
whatever.  k * 

It  is  not  his  design  to  gather  all  the  church 
into  one  place,  but  to  appoint  new  places  of 
gathering  from  time  to  time,  assembling  a 
few  thousand  at  a place  so  as  to  secure  a 
full  enjoyment  of  the  peculiar  rites  and 
ceremonies  of  his  church,  aqd  at  the  same 
time,  avoiding  those  jealousies  which  the 
assembling  of  the  whole  church  at  one 
place  naturally  engenders.  » 

He  has  no  connection  with  those  who 
have  recently  exercised  authority  in  Nau- 
voo,  but  regards  them  a3  usurpers.  On  the 
death  of  Joseph  Smith,  Strang  claimed  to  he 
his  successor  by  virtue  of  an  appointment 
from  Smith,  but  was  rejected  by  the  princi- 
pal men  in  the  church  with  so  much  prompt- 
ness that  most  of  the  church  did  not  hear  of 
him  at  all.  Poor,  sick  and  friendless,  but 
not  discouraged,  he  6at  down  quietly  to 
bide  his  lime  and  prepare  for  the  future. 
From  this  time  the  public  scarcely  heard  of 
him  till  the  first  of  January,  when  he  came 
out  with  the  first  number  of  the44  Voree 
Herald.’*  This  placed  him  antagonist  to 
those  in  authority  in  Nauvoo  in  almost  eve- 
ry point,  and  claiming  to  exercise  authority 
over  them,  he  immediately  followed  it  up 
by  summoning  the  principal  men  among 
them  before  him  to  answer  for  usurpation. 
Several  amoug  them,  including  two  of  the 
Twelve,  responded  to  the  summons,  ack- 
nowledged his  authority  and  are  now  preach- 
ing Strang  the  Prophet  with  great  success. 
His  friends  estimate  that  he  has  now  a 
majority  of  the  church  on  his  side.  Teams 
jure  crowding  to  the  new  place  of  gathering 
from  every  direction,  and  Voree  looks  more 
like  an  encampment  than  a town.  The 
Prophet  lives  in  a most  unostentatious  style. 


inn  room  eight  feet  by  twelve;  furnished 
with  a stove,  table  and  two  chairs.  This 
with  a small  sleeping  apartmehVmakes  the 
accomodation  for  him,  his  amiable  wife  and 
two  children.  Well  will  it  be  for  his  peo- 
ple if  they  do  not  make  him  proud  by  flatte- 
ry and  adulation. — Corres „ of  the  Ottawa

Seventies. 

According  to  the  Law  of  this  Church  there 
are  but  seven  quorums  of  seventies.  (D.  and  C. 
Sec.  3,  p.  43)  During  Brigham’s  usurpation  the 
number  has  been  run  up  to  33  or  34,  and  the 
Presidents  of  seventies  from  7 to  near  250.  A* 
the  Conference  at  Voree,  April  6th,  7th  and 
8th,  the  First  Presidency  wero  requested  to  set 
the  seventies  in  order  by  ascertaining  and  put- 
ting in  proper  place  all.  who  regularly  belong  to 
the  seventies,  and  will  take  their  place  and  fill- . 
ing  all  vacancies.  At  thg  Conference  near  St* 
Charles,  April  12th  and  13th,  the  question  camo 
up  what  was  the  effect  of  an  ordination  to  any 
quorum  of  seventy  above  the  seventh.  It  was 
held  that  the  ordination  teas  utterly  void,  be- 
cause it  was  to  a priesthood  which  God  had  not 
authorized  in  the  Church.  All  such  persons,  if 
found  proper  candidates  for  tho  priesthood, 
should  be  ordained  Elders,  Priests  or  Teachers. 
It  is  presumed  that  when  it  is  ascertained  what 
traveling  elder 8 really  do  belong  to  the  7 quo- 
rumns  of  70,  and  all  others  are  removed,  there 
will  bo  many  vacancies  which  will  be  rapidly 
filled  with  ciders  who  are  willing  to  travel  and 
preach  to  the  nations. 

This  is  the  only  course  that  can  be  pursued  in 
the  premises.  By  the  La\v  of  God  there  can  bo 
but  seven  seventies,  and  no  one  has  a right  to 
that  priesthood  except  he  travel  and  preach  to  the 
nations.  In  all  these  matters  wo  desire  to  fol 
low  only  the  Law  of  God,  fully  trusting  that 
when  lie  sees  the  need  of  more  than  7 seventies 
for  the  labor  of  the  vineyard  he  will  give  us  his 
word.  Those  who  havo  been  regiilarly  ordained 
to  the  seventies  and  cannot  continue  to  travel 
will  be  placod  in  the  elder’s  quorum.

Hyde’s  Revelation. 

Orson  Hyde  has  brought  forth  a pretended 
revalalion  as  a last  effort  to  put  down  the 
truth  and  sustain  his  apostacy,  and  for  the 
first  time  since  the  world  began  a pretended 
command  of  God  to  a whole  people  has 
come  forth  anonymously.  But  the  mark  of 
authorship  was  too  plain  to  keep  it  secret. 
Ilyde  has  not  only  acknowledged  it  in  public 
but  we  have  in  our  possession  a copy,  on 
the  back  of  which  he  writes  that  it  44 is 
original and  given  through”  him. 

We  learn  by  the  Book  of  Doctrine  and 
Covenants  that  no  one  shall  be  appointed  to 
the  gift  of  revalation  except  it  be  through 
Joseph;  and  this  shall  be  a law  unto  us 
that  we  receive  not  the  teachings  of  any 
not  thus  appointed,  as  revalations  or  com- 
mandments ; and  this  God  gave  us  that  we 
might  not  be  deceived,  that  we  might  know 
they  were  not  of  him,  (see  14th  p.  2.)-— 
Hyde  was  not  appointed  to  this  gift  by 
Joseph,  and  he  does  not  pretend  to  have 
been  so  appointed,  and  has  declared  before  a 
congregation  of  thousands  in  Nauvoo  that 
no  one  was,  and  never,  would  be.  These 
facts  speak  4br  themselves. 

The  revelation  itself  contains  several  com- 
mands the  import  of  all  which  is  that  the 
Saints . shall  not  investigate  doctrine  or 
principle , any  more,  but  gather  up  all  they 
have  and  remove  westward  without  de- 
lay. 

But  the  document  asserts  that  President 
44  Strang,  was  before  of  old  ordained  to 
gather  the  tares  of  the  field  and  that  the  an- 
gels have  chosen  him  to  do  it.”  44  But  his 
spirit  and  ambition  shall  soon  fail  him,  and 
he  shall  be  callbd  to  judgment”  Now  if 
Satan  helps  Hyde  as  he  Buys  he  helps 
Strang,  he  makes  most  bungling  work  ol  it 
here.  This  gathering  of  tares  does  not 
lake  place  till  the  wheat  is  .harvested. —

But  Hyde  says  (and  pretends  that  God 
said  iO  “ behold  James  J.  Strang  hath  cur-

who  as  ministers  of  the  gospel,  teach  that 
fornication  and-adultery  are  ordinances  of 
God’s  house.  Copies  of  that  curse  were 
then  circulated  in  Nanvoo  and  produced 
much  excitement  and  it  was  in  reference  to 
that,  that  Hyde  said  James  J.  Strang  hath 
not  cursed  my  people  by  my  spirit,  but

the  men  who  as  ministers  of  the  gospel 
teach  such  doctrinee  are  the  people  of  God 
and  that  God  does  not  curse  them  f Yes* 
that  is  just  what  he  means. 

Having  as  an  apostle  of  the  Lord  Jesns 
Christ  taught  such  doctrines  and  used  the 
authority  of  his  priesthood  to  enforce  and 
sanction  it,  he  was  a little  startled  at  the 
maledictions  of  God  on  his  own  head,  and 
attempts  to  turn  the  attention  of  the  people 
from  him  by  saying  that  Strang  has  cursed 
the  people  of  God. 

Well  here  is  the  curse.  Let  the  thou- 
sands who  have  in  the  past  10  months  wit- 
nessed its  workings  on  its  victims  judge 
whether  God  or  man  spoke  it.  44  As  for 
those  who,  as  gospel  ministers,  have  as- 
sumed to  teach  such  damning  soul  destroy- 
ing doctrines  (that  deceit,  fraud,Jying,  per- 
jury,  plundering  unbelievers,  for- 

nication and  adultery  are  required  oy  the 
command  of  God  in  the  upbuilding  of  his 
kingdom.)  In  the  name  of  God  and  the 
Lord  Jesus  Christ  ; may  their  bones  rot  in 
the  living  tomb  of  their  flesh;  may  their 
flesh  generate  fronv  its  own  corruptions  a 
loathsome  life  for  others  ; may  their  blood 
swarm  with  aleprouslife  of  motelike  ghast- 
ly corruption,  feeding  on  flowing  Hie,  gen- 
erating chilling  agues  and  burning  fevers. — 
May  peace  and  home  be  names  forgotten  to 
them,  and  the  beauty  they  have  betrayed  to 
infamy ; may  it  be  to  their  eyes  a'  crawling 
mass  of  putridity  and  battening  corruption,; 
its  delicate  hues  a sickly  light  that  glares 
from  universal  corruption;  its  auburn  tress- 
es the  posthumous  growth  of  temples*  of 
crawling  worms;  its  fragrant . breath  the 
blast  of  perdition.  Willi  desires  insatitate 
may  each  gratification  turn  to  burning  bit- 
terness and  glowing  shame. 

And  I prayed  onto  God  saying.  Oh  God 
curse  them  not,  and  let  me  not  raise  my 
voice  against  my  fellows.  But  he  said 
curse,  curse;  CURSE.  I will*  altogether

Conference  at  Vorec. 

Note, — The  sheets. containing  the  minutes  of 
the  Conferencojwd  the  resolutions  aro  mislaid  j 
and  os  we  are  now  almost  ready  to  go  to  press, 
must  delay  their  publication  tiU  the  next  num- 
ber. Tho  following  is  a synopsis  of  tho  pro- 
ceedings : 

On  the  sixth  day  of  April,  1840,  Con* 
ference  assembled  at  Voree. 

Present— James  J.  Strang,  first  Presi- 
dent; Aaron  Smith,  Counsellor;  John  E. 
Page,  of  the  Twel  ve ; sixteen  Highpriests , 
and  a large  number,  of  Elders,  Prieslsf 
Teachers,  &c. 

James  J.  Strang  was  appointed  President 
of  the  Conference — and  Reuben  Miller, 
Clerk. 

Resolutions  were  unanimously  passed, 
sustaining  the  President,  his  counsellor 
and  all  the  authorities  and  quorums  of  the 
church,  which  remain  in  the  order  establish- 
ed by  revelation,  providing  for  setting  the 
rest  in  order,  and  putting  under  admoni- 
tion and}  suspense,  every  person  holding 
any  priesthood  in  this  Church  who  is  in 
rebellion  against  the  Presidency  and  the 
order  of  Church  government  established 
by  God . 

A High  Council  was  organized,  for  the 
trial  of  Brigham  Young , Heber  C.  Kim- 
ball, Orson  Hyde, , Parley  P.  Frail,  John 
Taylor,  Willard  Richards  and  George  Jl. 
Smith,  all  members  of  the  quorum  of  the 
Twelve. 

They  were  put  on  trial  on  charges  of 
which  the  following  is  an  abstract : 

Conspiracy  to  overthrow  the  order  of  the 
Church. 

1.  By  teaching  by  counsel  and  com- 
mandment that  Jos.  Smith,  President,  <fcc„ 
of  the  Church  was  to  have  no  suc- 
cessor. 

2.  Forbidding  to  examine  and  to  hear 
teachings  or  arguments  on  that  question. 

3.  By  assuming  that  the  Twelve  as 
Apostles,  have  power  to.  dictate  all  the  af- 
fairs of  the  Church  in  all  the  world  with- 
out a presidency  to  direct  them. 

4.  By  denouncing  excommunication  and 
the  curses  of  God  on  all  who  disbelieved 
or  examined  their  pretensions. 

5.  By  driving  with  threats  and  violence 
from  the  church^and  the  city  of  Nauvoo 
such  as  would  pot  vote  for  their  .mea- 
sures. 

. Usurpation. 

1.  Exercising  authority  which  belongs 
exclusively  to  Uie  First  Presidency. 

2.  Exercising  powers  belonging  exclu- 
sive to  the  High  Council. 

3.  Taking  tq  themselves  the  powers  and 

‘'duties  of  the  lesser  quorums.  - 

4.  Assuming  jurisdiction  over  president 
James  J.  Strang  and  Coun.  Aaron  Smith, 
over  whom  they  could  .have  none. 

6.  Selling  and  offering  to  sell  church 
property  without  authority,  find  converting 
the  same  to  their  individual  use. 

0*  Commanding  the  church,  in  the 


Jgroo  of  God  to  go  into  the  ~witdeir- 
nese.  * . 

7.  Giving  a pretended  endowment  with- 
out authority  and  altogether  out  of  order. 

- Tfyrtmnpju*  Administration. 

Disciplining  and  excommunicating 
members  of  the  church  who  are  charged 
with  no  crime,  without  any  notice  of  their 
intended  proceedings,  and  without  witness- 
es or  proof  of  any  kind  against  them. 

& Unlawful  collections  of  tithing*. 

3.  Abolishing  the  liberty  of  speech  and 
the  press  by  command  and  violence, 

4.  Systematically  plundering  the  church 
of  their  property  for  the  use  of  themselves 
and  their  favorites. 

Teaching  False  Dotirina*

3.  That  the  Bible,  Book  of  Mormon 
and  Book  of  Doctrines  and  Covenants  are 
of  no  binding  force  in  tho  church, 

4.  That  lying,  to  build  op  lho  Church 
of  God,  is  justifiable,  and  that  secret  wick* 
educes  will  not  be  brought  into  judgment; 
and  carrying  out  those  principles  in  con- 
stant practice. 

Blasphemy. 

1 . Cursi  ng  the  prophet  of  God  appoint- 
ed to  be  their  leader,  and  all  who  acknow- 
ledge him. 

2.  Teaching  that  weapons  of  war  and 
violence  are  the  preparation  of  the  Gospel 
of  Peace,  the  first  principles  of  the  Gospel 
and  the  gift  of  the  Holy  Ghost. 

Separate  charge  against  Orson  Hyde. 

Falsely  pretending-  to  a 'revelation  from 
God. 

The  testimony  taken  was  very  volumin- 
ous, and  fully  sustained  all  tho  charges 
against  Young,  Kimball  and  Hyde;  and 
the  principal  part  of  them  against  all  tho 
persons  on  trial. 

After  a full  hearing,  and  tho  remarks  of 
six  members  of  the  Council,  President 
Strang  pronounced  the  xmanimous  judg- 
ment of  the  Council  that  Brigham  Young , 
Heber  C.  Kimball , Orson  Hyde,  Parley 
P.  Pratt , John  Taylor , IHllard  Rich- 
ards, and  George  Smith , be  excommu- 
nicated from  the  Church  of  Jesus  Christ 
of  iMlter  Day  Saints,  and  be  del  hired 
over  to  the  bufferings  of  Satan  in  the 
flesh. 

Tho  proceedings  of  the  High  Council 
in  the  premises  were  submitted  to  the

Correspondence. 

Cincinnati,  March  23d,  1842. 
Dear  Brother  : 

I rejoice  to  hear  that  we  have  a Prophet, 
and  embrace  this  as  a suitable  opportunity 
of  addressing  you  a few  lines  informing  yon 
of  the  state  of  the  Church  of  Jesus  Christ 
of  Latter  Day  Saints  in  this  place.  Here- 
tofore there  has  been  two  parties  in  this 
place,  one  being  for  Rigdon  and  the  other 
for  the  old  organization.  But  on  the  recep- 
tion of  the  first  number  of  the  Voree  Her- 
ald the  parties  became  united,  and  I think 
there  is  neither  a "man  nor  a woman  in  the 
place  for  Rigdon.  The  branch  meets  togeth- 
er in  union,  and  there  are  but  one  or  two 
persons  that  I know  of  who  are  in  favor  of 
the  California  expedilion. 

We  have  about  eight  eldere  in  this 
place  including  one-  High  Priest.  And  1 
will  take  the  liberty,  of  asking  you  if  it 
would  not  be  advisable  to  hold  a conference 
at  a period  not  long  hence.  We  are  very 
much  at  a loss  to  know  what  course  to  pur- 
sue at  this  time,  and  should  any  of  the  el- 
ders with  whom  yoir  are  acquainted  be 
passing  we  would  take  it  as  a great  favor  if 
they  would  call  upon  us,  as  I assure  you 
they  would  meet  with  a favorable  recep- 
tion. 

* I would  make  further  enquiry  of  you  rel- 
ative to  the  town  of  Voree,  whether  it  is 
laid  out  yet,  and  if  it  is  your  will,  do  me  a 
kindness  by  giving  me  a descripton  of  the 
iots,_theix  jsize,  and  present  value  &c.»  as 
the  members  here  feel  desirous. of  obtaining 
an  inheritance  before  it  falls  into  speculators 
hand:?; and  we  are  desirous  of  knowing  the 


Nauvoo,  Feb.  1st,  1846. 
Mr.  James  J;  Strang: 

Sir;  I have  read  your  paper  of  January 
“ the  Voree  Herald;”  As  far  as  the  theory 
of  your  claims,  as  set  forth  in  your  paper 
and  epistles  is  concerned,  it  is,  most  unques- 
tionably correct;  and  the  only  question  re- 
maining with  me  isu , whether  or  not  your 
claims  are  true  in  your  case.  My  prayer 
is  that  the  God  of  Iisrael  may  deign  to  stoop, 
to  unworthy  me,  and  bear  tidings  to  me  in 
your  case ; as  once  he  did  in  the  case  of 
President  Smith  and  the  book  of  Mormon. 
When  I gain  that  blessing,  I shall  as  fear- 
lessly advocate  your  cause  as  I did  Presi- 
dent Smith’s. 

That  such  an  appointment  is  necessary, 
has  been  settled  with  me  for  more  than  three 
months  past,  consequently  I have  taken  lit- 
tle or  no  interest  is  the  councils  of  the 
church.  In  the  absence  of  the  first  presi- 
dency, I Have  looked  on  the  church  as  being 
like  a clock  without  weights,  or  a watch 
without  a mainspring,  all  stops  till  they 
are  restored,  seeing  the  commandments  al- 
ready given,  has  provided  that  “ the  Twelve 
are  to  act  under  the  direction  of  the  first 
presidency the  question  with  me  is,  how 
can  they  act  in  this  office  legally  without , 
or  in  the  absence  of  the  first  Presidency ,” 
which  presidency  being  necessary  through 
“ necessity ,”  “ agreeable  to  the  institutions 
of  heaven .” 

These  reflections,  together  with  an  in- 
numerable train  of  like  nature,  connected 
with  them,  which  1 have  not  space  on  a 
single  sheet  to  state,  have  thrown  my  mind 
into  a state  that  is  almost  indescribable.-^ 
Suffice  it  to  say,  that  my  bosom  has  heaved 
with  pain  and  anxiety  day  and  night,  my 
mind  more  or  less  has  been  en  veloped  in  a 
gloom  to  which  I was  an  entire  stranger 
while  President  Smith  lived,  and  yet  my 
mind  has  been  as  clear,  and  as  rfeutc  on 
the  subject,  nature,  and  principles  of  the 
faith  as  ever  it  was  since  1 embraced  it. — 
Finding  no  succesor  of  President  Smith 
appointed  by  him  according  to  the  revala- 
tions  already  given,  and  knowing  the  ille- 
gallily , of  the  subaltern  authorities,,  moving 
forward  to  effect  the  work  that  God  design- 
ed in  the  setting  up  of  this  church , I have 
been  thrown  into  a dillemma  of  mind,  from 
which!  wish  to  be,  in  the  due  time  of  tho 
Lord,  extricated.  * 

For  surely  if  President  Smith  "did  not 
“ appoint”  a J*r6phett  Revalator,  Trans, 


U 


A 


laior  and  Seer”^  in  'hisstead,  the  whole 
work  has  come  to'a  dead'stand^and  ajoum- 
cd  proceedings  sini  die  (in  a legal  point  of 
light.) 

The'  last  day  of  May  next  will  be  ten 
years  jsi  nee  I first  left  my  family,  and  my 
jitile  all,  to  preach.,  the  everlasting  gospel. 
From  that  time  until  about  one  year  since,  I 
have  beetTincessantly  employed  in  the  vin- 
yard,  and  have  baptized  more  than  one 
thousand  souls.  I began  "my  work-in  ex- 
treme poverty  and  have  suffered  every  pri- 
vation imaginable.  My  family  have  gone 
through  untold  Sufferings  in  my  absence, 
and  1 am  yet  as  poor  and  destitute  as  when 
I first  entered  into  the  ministry.  I have 
served  this  people  in  all  diligence  for  ten 
years  past,  thinking  most  implicitly,  that 
whenever  I should  return  to  any  of  the 
stakes  of  Zion  to  settle,  my  labors  would  be 
appreciated  by  the  church  and  its  authori- 
ties, & I be  sustained  in  my  capacity  equal 
with  my  brethren  of  the  same  calling. 

I arrived  in  this  place  one  year  ago  the 
21st  day  of  December,  poor  and  destitute  of 
this  world’s  goods,  at  least  to  that  degree 
necessary  to  occupy  my  place  in  the  quo- 
rum to  which  I belong. 

I find  my  jet  black  hair  faded,  to  a silver 
gray,  my  lungs,  once  like  a Lion’s/ are  now 
hoarse  and  faltering,  my  bodily  system, 
once  like  iron  and  steel  for  strength,  is  now 
more  like  a child’s  ; soft  and  miserably  in- 
firm. I have  a wife  and  three  children  ; my 
wife  is  now  sick  and  confined  to  her 
bed. 

Infirm,  I am  reduced,  to  the  capacity  of  a 
mere  "menial  servant;  and  to  the  mostsevere 
hardships  to  keep  body  and  soul  together. 

Tluit  saying  of  “ bleating  like  sheep  by. 
day,  and  devouring  like  wolves  by  night,’’ 
God  knows  does  not'  apply  to  me  however 
much  it  may* to  others;  I leave  others  to 
speak  for  themselves,  one  thing  I do  know, 
which  is  this,  my  brethren  of  the  same 
quorum  appear  to  enjoy  a respnable  plenty 
to  sustain  them  in  their  capacity.  I do  not 
sav  they  have  Too  much,  but  I do  say,  that 
I do  not  enjoy  enough.  I have  always  ac- 
ted upon  the  principles  of  open,  plain,  frank 
and  punctual  reciprocity,  expecting  for  my 
services,  that  others  would  tinder  a sense  of 
duty,  serve  me" in  return,  with  a sulficincy 
at  least,  to  sustain ‘me  in  the  faithful  and 
punctul  discharge  of  the-dnties  of  my  capaci- 
city.  I have  never  studied  any  intrigue- 
ing  craft  to  gain  means,  for  a livelihood,  and 
I now  think  it  loo  late  in  the  day  to  begin. 
If  I cannot  be.  sustained  in  this  way,  I must 
most  assuredly  fail  in  accumulating  a sub-: 
s stance.  If  ever  my  present  bands  of  re- 
straint are  cut  asunder,  and  I get  out  from 
under  the  influence  and  power  of  a partial 
despotism,  I am  fully  resolved,  to  let  loose 
my  small  artillery  in  an  open  war  of  words 
against  false  principles,  not  private  ch:ir- 
acter,  for  I am  fully  aware,  that  the  expo- 
sure of  private  character,  never  lends  to  a 
reformation  ; but  correct  principles  form 
•correct  characters. 

I believe  in  the  policy  of  the  freedom  of 
speech,  and  the  pre3s;  with  the  full  assu- 
rance, that  truth  “ dimond  truth ” will  pre- 
vail. If  I am  ever  led  to  receive  you,  or' 
itay  other  man  as*  PresL  Smithy  succesor, 
I shall  most  assuredly,  improve  the  first 
opportunity  of  declaring  my  principles,  rel- 


ative to  usages  of  the  church of  one  mem- 
ber towards  another,  nhd  the  reciprocal  du- 
ties of  the  officers  to  members  and  the  mem- 
bers to  the  officers. 

' JOHN  E.  PAGE. 

One  of  the  'Twelve  of  the  Church  of  Je- 
sus Christ  ofLatter  Day  Saints. 

Nauv6o,  March  11,  1846. 
Brother  Strang : 

I have  perused  with  becoming  interest 
your  several  letters  sent  lo  my  mother  and 
sister  Emma;  also  some  of  your  papers 
with  your  remarks  on  the  order  of  the 
church,  which  clearly  evinces  the  true 
spirit  of  old  Mormonism  as  far  as  l can 
discern  the  faith  and  doctrine  that  I have 
been  advocating  for  years,  and  for  which,  of 
late  my  family  (mother  Smith  not  excepted) 
have  been  disfranchised  from  the  church  (as 
they  call  it)  by  the  Twelve,  and  much 
abused  by  their  infatuated  followers.  Time 
would  fail  me  to  mention  ni  l-  of -the  accu- 
mulated wrongs  they  have  inflicted  upon  a 
poor  and  helpless  family,  whose  members 
have  mostly  fallen  by  the  band  of  a ruth- 
less mob  and  the  treachery  of  false  hearted 
brethren.  A few  yst  remain  to  suffer  by 
the  falsehoods  heaped  upon  them,  and  the 
confiscation  of  their  goods.;  their  rights  of 
church  property  taken  from  them,  until  the 
bleeding  heart  of  an  aged  mother  wrung 
with  anxiety  & disgust  sinks  with  anguish, 
and  faints  at  the  thoughts  of  a recital  of  the 
awful  tale.  Hear  it,  O ye  Latter  Day 
Saints:  your  Mother  in  Israel,,  who  oft- 
times  has  pursed  you  at  her  side,  and  with 
her  motherly  care  and  teaching  comforted' 
your  hearts,  must  now  be  driven  from  your 
midst,  penniless — robbed  of  her  inheritance 
in  the  city  of  Joseph  by  the  cruelty  of  your 
‘ rulers.  ~ 

On  yesterday  we  were  told  by  a commit- 
tee of  two,  a Mr.  Babbit  and  a Mr.  Hay- 
wood, that  unless  we  would  .acknowledge 
the  Twelve  as  the  heads  of  the  church, 
Mother  Smith  could  have  no  inheritance  in 
Nauvoo.  This,  they  said,  was  the  counsel 
of  the  church  whom  they  represent . 

We  are  branded  also  with  the  cpilliot  of 
apostates  by  these  men,  to  drive  us  from 
the  church  and  trample  us  under  their  feet ; 
they  assuming  the  entire  control  of  the 
church,  regardless  of  religion,  Of  rights  and 
the  laws  of  God  : regardless,  also,  of  all 
our  labofs  in  the  church  for  years  gone 

I shall,  if  the  Lord  will,  visit  your  place 
before  long,  and  would  he  glad  to  attend 
your  conference  had  I the  means  of  doing 
.so.  You  may/ be  assured  that  we  Tire 
thankful  for  every  kind  word. 

My  mother  and  family  in  general  join 
with  me rin  sending  their  love  to  you  and 
all  the  saints  scattered  abroad. 

We  shall  all  leave  this  place  (Nauvoo) 
for  some  more  heavenly  land — the  Lord 
kimw.s  where*  fur  1 believe  lie  will  gather 
the  pure  in  heart  anil  save  them  from  fur- 
ther ruin. 

I have  not  time  to  write  more . I would 
be  glad  to  hear  from  you  as  soon  as  conve- 
nient. Adieu — -may  the  God  of  love  and 
peace" direct  air  your  footsteps  and  bring  us 
'at  last  unto  his  heavenly  kingdom/  . 

WM*  SMITH, 

one  of  the.  Twice  and  Patriarqk. 


Movement  of  ths  Mormons.— The  M or 
mon  Expedition i9  ndvf  encamped  about 40 
miles  from  Keoaauqua,  Iow«v  about  fifty 
miles  from  Nauvoo.  From  their  encamp- 
ment empty  wagons  are  daily  returning  to 
Nauvoo  and  some  persons  have  returned  on 
loot.  ..The  notorious  Orrin  P.  RockwelL 
and  Jack  Redding  have  returned.  On  their 
way  being  asked  why  they  came  back, 
they  said  they  were  after  some  scalps . 

The  Mormons  have  now^been  encamped 
at  Kcosauqua  several  days.  Their  meuiP 
hire  themselves  out  to  the  farmers  in  ihcr 
neighborhood,  and  seem  disposed  to  remain 
for  some  time.  There  is  some  mystefy  in 
this  movement,  and  much  curiosity  to  know 
wind  it  means.  We  suspect  that  the  se- 
cret lies  here:  when  the  Twelve  arrived  at 
Iveosauqua  they  learned  that  Bill  Smith  had 
returned  and  was  figuring  largely  at  Nau- 
voo. They  also  learned  that  the  Strang- 
ites  had  gained  considerable  strength  after 
they  left.  They  therefore  determined  to 
halt  and  send  hack  empty  wagons  for  more 
provisions  and  also  send  back  llveir  bullies, 
Rockwell  and  Redding  to  frighten  certain 
obnoxious  persons  out  af  Nauvoo; — Platt* 
vilie  American.* 

Fruit. — A farmer  whb  is  famous  for 
good  fruit,  says  he  raises  his  trees  in  the 
following  manner.  lie  takes  a culling 
from  the  best  tree  he  can  find,  fits  the  end 
of  a cutting  into  a large  potato,  and  sets  it 
in  the  earth,  leaving  but  one  or  two  inches 
of  the  cutting  above  the  ground.  The  cut-

Ordination. 

Who  should  ordain  the  successor  of  Jo- 
seph Smith  ? Not  Joseph,  for  oo  bat  ono 
can  hold  tho  office  at  the  name  time,  and  as  ev- 
ery man  has  his  priesthood  from  tha~mDment  of 
ordination,  so  if  Joseph  ordained  him,  that  very 
moment  ho  \yould  lose  his  own  station.  But  it 
is  not  true  that  any  man  Iojos  Priesthood  in  this 
Church  by  doing  his-  duty.  Consoqthmtfy  it  is 
not  the  President1*  duty  to  ordain  hio  successor; 
it  is  not  the  duty  of  his  counsellors  in  tho  Pres- 
idcncy,  nor  of  tho  Twelve,  for  they. are  all  be- 
low him,  and  the  less  cannot  bless  tho  greater. 
How  then  ? “Ilo  that  is  ordained  of-  God  shall 
come  in  at  the  gate,  (appointment  by  revelation) 
and  he  ordained  as  he  has  told.ua  before.”  (D. 
& C.,  Sec.  14,  p;  2.)  How  did  God  tell  us  before? 
“God  ministered  unto  him  by  an  holy  angel 
whoso  countenaneo  was  as  lightning,  and  whnao 
garments  were  pure  white,  abovo  all  whiteness, 
and,  gavo  unto  him  commandments  which  in- 
spired him  and  gavo  him  power  from  On  high.” 

[ D.  C.  sec.  2,  p.  2.]

New  Paper  at  Nauvoo. 

Wo  have  just  got  our  eyes  on  the  first  and

__  ___  The  Temples. 

At  the  April  Conference  of  the  Church  of  Je- 
sus Christ  of  Latter  Dky  Saints,  resolutions 
were  passed  unanimously  protesting  against  the 
sale  of  the  Templet  at  Nauvoo  and  Kirtland, 
and  expressing  the  opinion  that  the  men  who 
profess Xq  ho  trustees  in  trust  at  Nauvoo  are  not 
legally  m office  and  have  no  right  to  convoy  title 
to  any  property  of  the  Church*.  We  caution  all 
against  purchasing  church  property  of  them*. 

The  question  is  frequently  asked  why  did*  not 
Mr.  Strang  come  forward  with  his  claims  before? 
The  question  is  asked  in  ignorance  of  the  facts. 
Mr.  Strang  has  claimed  his  place  as.  successor  of 
Joseph  Smith-  from  his  death,  till  the  present 
time,,  artdhas  devoted,  himself  untiringly  to  the 
bringing  forward  of  that  claim.  It  is  others 
who  have  kept  tho  church  in  ignorance  on  the 
subject. 

How  can  Brigham  or  any  other  man  have  the 
effrontery  to  say  that  Joseph  was  not  to  have  a 
successor  unless  he  fell  ? “And  this  ye  shall 
know  assuredly,  that  there  is  none  other  appoint- 
ed unto  you  to  receive  commandments  and  reve- 
lations until  nE  be  TAKEN  if  he  abide  in  me. 
(D.  and  G^Bcc.  1-4,  p.  1)  For  I have  given  unto 
him  the  keys  of  mysteries  and  revelations  until 
I shall  appoint  unto  them  another  in  his  stead . — 
(D  & C.,  Sec.  51,  p.  2.) 


TO  the  Saints  in  Hancock  County. 

Beloved  Brethren  : 

As  many  inquiries  have  been  "made  of  mo  by 
letter  and  otherwise,  what  you  ought . to  do  in 
your  present  perils,  especially  in  regard  to  dis- 
posing of  your  lands  there,  and  gathering  to 
Voree,  I havo  thought  proper  to  address  this 
public  epistle  to  you  all.  Where  you  havo 
doubtful  and  uncertain  titles  to  your  lands  it  is 
advisable  that  to  avoid  litigation  and  violence 
you  sell  them  at  what  they  will  fetch  ; and  that 
you  profer  to  sell  bn  the  samo  torms  to  the  ad- 
verse claimant,  rather  than  any  other  person,  be- 
causo  that  will  leave  peace  behind  you,  as  well 
as  bring  it  with  you.  Where  your  titles  are 
good,  continually  offer  the  lands  for  sale  at  pri- 
ces decidedly  moderate,  until  you  get  a bargain 
but  don’t  give  away  your  lands.  If  you  cannot 
sell  at  all,  rent  your  lands  on  the  best  terms  you 
can ; so  that  they  ore  taken  care  of,  and  you 
havo  means  to  come  to  Voreo.  If  you  have  not 
the  means  of  coming  to  Voree,  but  can  move 
part  way,  take. the  Mississippi  route  and  seek 
employ  in  tho  minoral  country  ; or  the  Illinois 

utc  and.seck  employ  on  tho  Illinois  and  Michi- 
i Canal,  and  among  tho  farmers,  tilt  you  can- 
gamer  with  your  brethren. 

BuLrif  you  cannot,  in  any  honest  way,  get  tho 
moansTof  leaving  Hancock  County,  go  to  work 
there,'  4iko  industrious  peaceable  citizens.  Come 
as  ooqn^as  you  are  able ; but,  until  then,  neither 
fight  nor  run.  If  men  put  torches  to  your  hou- 
ses, don’t  hurt  them.  If  they  threaten  to  kill 
you,  don’t  run  from  them.  Non  resistance  is 
astrenger  defenr  * than  all  the  artillery  on  earth 


If  your  enemies  smith  you  bn  ono  ebook,  turn  to 
them  the  o$her. 

In  selling  lands  you  may  consider  good  cattle, 
and  harass,-  fit  for  immediate  service,  as  good  ao 
cash  at  six  months.  All  kinds  .of  property  is 
good  at  its  value  at  Voree,  except  guns  and 
Watches.  We  are  too  poor  to  purchase  watches 
and  too  peaceable  to  need  guns,  and  neither  will 
buy  land  of  unbelievers.  Nearly  all  kinds  of 
personal  property  which  yottmay  have-on  hand 
will  bear  transportation  to  this  place.^  * 

JAMES  J..  STRANG. 

Voree,  April  1846. 

Prospectus  for  the  Voree  Herald. 

The  Presidency  of  the  Church  of  Jesus  Christ  * 
of  Latter  Day  Saints  knowing  the  scattered  and 
divided  condition  of  the  Church,  and  realizing 
the  anxiety  of  all  the  saints  to  learn  of  our  con- 
dition and  prospects,  have  determined  on  tho  1 
-publication  of  a periodicalat  Voreer  the  present  F 
seat  of-tho-Presidenoy* — - - 

The  Voree  Herald  will  be  devoted  to  tho  in- 
culcation of  the  true  doctrines  of  the  Gospel,, 
the  revealed  order  of  the  Church,'  and  a Bound 
healthy  system  of  morals.  It  wilFalso  contain  j; 
a summary  of  the  acts  and  proceedings  of  tho 
Church,  and'  information  of  the  progress  of 
evangelical  truth,  from  time  to  time..  ] 

Tho  present  is  a new  and  important  era  in  tho 
history  of  this  Church’,  God  has,  in  his  own 
mysterious  manner,  preserved  her  through  her 
trials,  Slie  is  just  emerging  from-  a night  of' 
darknoss.  A brighter  day!  is  dawning  than  sho 
has  yet  hadr  Not  only  the  renovation  of  tho 
Church,  buf^the  sending:  but  of  elders  to  all  tho 
four  quarters  of  the  earth,  and  tho  building,  up 
of  a new  stake  give  importance  to  the  times,, 
and  an  additional  interest  to  the  proceedings  of 
tho  saints. 

Considering  the  divisions  which  hare  grown’ 
up  among  .the  brethren,  and  the  great  errors 
which  havo  been  imbibed  merely  by  neglect  of 
investigation,  we  hope  the  brethren  will  take 
warning  and  all  subscribe  for  the  only  publication 
of  theSzhurch  on  the  whole  continent.^  A church 
of  100,000  members  surely  can  support  ono  pa-~ 
per. 

The  Herald  is  at  present  issued  once  a month, 
but  a3  soon  as  wo  can  fit  up  an  offico.jwg  shall  ' 
issue  it  twice  a month.  It  will  be  furnished  to 
subscribers  at  one  dollar  a year,  payable  in  all 
cases  in  advance.  Thrce^  numbers  have* already 
.been  issued  and  we  have  on  hand  a few  copies 
which  will  be  furnishod  to  the  subscribers  whose 
n^mes  first  come  in.  Money  may  be  forwarded 
by  mail  according  to  tho  Post  office  regulations, 
and  always  postage  paid,  otherwise  the  letters 
may  not  be  taken  from  the  office.

VOREE,  .TUNE,  1846. 

H/^To  Correspondents.  VVe  have  many 
communications  on  our  hands  which  we  de- 
sire to  publish,  but  have  not  been  able  to  find 
room.  In'our  next  we  hope  to  insert  a no- 
tice of  several  conferances,  especially  those 
-in  Nauvoo  and  Knox  Co.  Ills. 

QT/^Our  beloved  brother  Wm.  Smith 
one  of  the  Twelve  and  the  only  survi- 
ving brother  of  the  martyred  Prophet, 
arrived  in  Voree  with  Bishop  Wm. 
Marks  a few  days  since.  He  is  in  good 
health  and  spirits;  and  is  making  arrange- 
ments to  erect  a house  for  his  mother  on 
a lot  which  a gentleman  in  the  place  has 
given  him  for  that  purpose.  Br.  Wil- 
liam will  be  ordained  to  the  office  of  Pa 
iriarch  to  the  whole  church  under  the 
hands  ofthefirst  Presidency  according  to 
his  inheritance  and  the  blessing  of  his 
father. 

Character  of  Nauvoo. 

This  is  a ticklish  subject  to  speak  on,  but  so 
many  errors  prevail  that  wo  can  not  excuse 
ourselves  if  we  do  not  try  to. correct  them. . 
We  spent  a short  time  there  in  the  winter 
and  spring  of  1844  and  found  it  the  most  in- 
dustrious, peaceful  and  orderly  city  weever^ 
visited.  In  a city  of  15,000  inhabitants, 
there  was  doubtless  more  or  less  of  crime, 
and'there  was  occasionally  seen  a sprink- 
ling of  that  dangerous  population  which  in- 
fests every  town  on  the  Mississippi ; but  the 
place  appered  toi  be  neither  a home  or  a re- 
fuge to  them.  When  the  troubles  began 
there  iu  June  1844,  many  of  that  class  doubt- 
less come  and  joined  hands  with  both  parties 
(as.  they  do  in  ©very  such  fray,)  not  because 
they  cared  for  either  party,  but  because  in 
times  of  violence  there  was  opportunity  for 
plunder. 

The  following  wintor  the  Charter  of  Nau- 
voo was  taken  awayjind  the  city,  containing 
probably.  15,000  inhabitants,  left  without  a 
government.  That  under  such  circmstanccs 
it  should  become  the  refuge  and  rendesvoux 
of  the  immence  gang  of  plunders  & blacklgs, 
who  prowl  up  and  down  the  Mississippi,  wus 
a mere  matter  of  course.  And  so  it  was. 
And  Brigham,  changing  altogether  to  works 
. of  wickedness  became  tho  leador>  of.  that 
band,  upon  the  same  principle  that  the  arch- 
angel fallen  became'  prince  of  Devils. — 
First  in  good  if  altogether  corrupted,  becomes 
first  in  wickedness.  That  he  and  several,  of 
his,  principal  associates  are  as  corrupt  men 
ss  can  be  found  on  earth,  is  beyond  question, 
but  they  have  had  the  shrewdness  to  conceal 
their  conduct  from  theTmasses.  These  act 
as  leaders-  ia  the  western  emigration  and  are 
surrounded  by  strong  bands  of  the  most  des- 
perate and  dangerouslnen  to  be  found  in  the 
wester n valley,  a very  large  majority  of 


& whom  were never  members  'of  the  \Churck 
' •armed  to  vthn  teeth  arid,  always  ready  iot  a 
. .fray. 4/  , With  - thorn  ] have  he  al s go n e' 
_ some  hundreds  oflrespectable  citizensrmany 
. ?f  . whom  are  now  no  better  off  than  slaves 
and. are  kept  from  returning  by  a numerous 
armed, guard.5  They  do  riot|recognizW the  au- 
thority of  the  United  States*  Bnngham, 
"ioung  and  a few  associates  ex  ercise  the 
power  oU life  and* death,  j " - 

Such  are  the  fruits  of  ecclesiastical  usur- 
udtion  and  Legislative  imbecility.  There  is 
no  excuse  for  the  taking  from  Nauvoo  its 
charter.  1 f the  charter . was  abused,  Jet  them 
so  alter  it  as  to  prevent  abuse.  If  need 
he,  they  could  give  the  appointed  , of  all 
city  officers  to  the  ' Governor  and’ "Senate,, 
and  require  that  they  be  selected  from  dis- 
tant parts  of  the  State.  ‘ But  in'  refusing 
them  a government  they  opened  tlie  flood- 
gates of  vice,  and  invited  civil' war.  The 
public  press  in  .charging  the  corruptions  of 
that  place  on  the  masses  does  most  gross  in- 
justice. 

In  our  last  number  we  published  part  of; 
Gov.  Fords  letter  on  the  exile  of  the  Mor- 
mons from  Illinois.  lit  will,  he  perceived 
that  he  confesses  his  . inability  “to  see  to  the 
execution  of  the  laws.'”  .We  think'  he  must  be 
a little  humiliated  in  making  the  confession. 
That  a Gov.  should  acknowledge  himself 
unable  to  protect  the  citizens  of  part  of  a 
State,  or  to  enforce  the  laws  in  a particular 
county,  shows  either  great  imbecility  or  a 
very  unsound  public  opinion,.  ‘ Inthiscase 
we  attribute  it  to  the  latter;  but  we  think  the 
Go\\  would , have  shown  his  wisdom  in 
mrching  an  armed  force  into  Hancock  Co  . 
sufficiently  strong  to  put  down  all  opposition, 
and  arresting  every * violator  of  the  law  of 
what  ever  party,  especially  all  who  opposed 
the  execution  of  the  laws.  If  he  could  not 
get  the  force  within  the  State  he  could  ob- 
tain the  aid  of  the  U.  S.  force. 

And  we  think -the  Legislature  would  have 
shown  more  \yisdom  if  instead  of  abolishing 
law  in  Hancock  Co.  it  had  rtferely  with- 
drawn favoritism , and  passed  laws  suitable 
to  the  emergency,  throwing  the  whole  ad- 
ministration of  both  City  and  Co.  if  necessa- 
ry into  the  hands  of  distinguished  civillians 
from  distant  parts  of  the  State.  If  people 
can  not  be  found  in  the  county  to  govern  it 
by  law , we  think  ill  pf  trusting  them  to  gov- 
ern it  without  law.  These  thing  are  ulcers 
upon  our  Republican  institutions.  When 
shall  we  see  the  end  of  them? 

The  Apostate  Camp. — The  avowed  ob- 
ject of  the  camp  in  leaving  the  U.  S.  was  to 
sot  up  an  independent  government.  The 
plan  was  to  send  round  to  Californ  ia  from 
England  and  N.  Y some  ten  thousand  emi- 
grants, and  to  lead  out  by  land  to  the  Mis- 
souri River  twenty  five  thousand.  These 
were  tobe  established  at  the  favorable  points 
across  the  continent  frormCouncil  Bluff  to  the 
Bay  of  St.  Francisco,  and  to  act  in  conjunct- 
ion with  the  Indians  in  setting  up  an  immense' 
empire.  Among  other  provisions  for  this 
enlerprize  the  camp  which  left  Nnuvoo  took 
with  them  seventeen  cannon  and'an  arma- 
mont  sufficient  for  five  times  as  many  men, 
as  went  out.  . The  difficulties  of  so  great 
an  undertaking,  the  want  of  intelligent  lea- 
ders and  the  unexpected  opposition  they  have 


•]  met.  ^rgm  ^his^ quarter,*  has  reduced  their 
3 ope  tenth' what  they  expected. 

| of  still  in  their  preparation  has 

* already* red uced  these  to  a state  of  Starva- 
tion. The  presence  of  a very  few  U.  S. . 
troops  on  the  Missouri  has  rendered;  it  dfli- 
cult  for  them  to  pass  with  their  armament. — 
And  finally  , the  prospect  of  California  falling 
into  the  hands  of  the  U.  S.  has  broken  upgall 
their  plans.  They  are  yet' in  the  western 
part  of  Iowa  and  the  leaders  Jiave  not  only 
sloped  there  for  the  season  but  confess  them- 
selves-at  a.  loss  which  way  to  go.  .They  are 
making  an  ^effort-  to  possdheir  cannon  ser 
cretly  beyond  theJU.  S.  torts, Tut, the  suffering 
is  so  great,  desertion  so  frequent  and  the  pros- 
:>pect  of  accomplishing  their,  ultimate  object 
so  unpromising.thaUno  change  would  sup  rise 
us.  Orson -Pratt  has  been  trying  to^cut 
loose;  but  .they  would  let  him  return  only  on 
leaving  this  family  and  property.  * 

They  have  been  generally  unsuccessful  in 
their  ^ negotiations  with  the  Indians.  The- 
probabilily  is  that  more  tribes  are  .hostile  to 
them  "thenTriendly.. If  no  opposition  should 
interpose  itself  except  from  the  Indians  we; 
do' not  believe  the  camp  would  ever  cross  the  .7 
mountains,  as  it  is  we  expect  to  hear  of  their, 
beggars  going  through  the;  country  to  get 
help  for  them  to  save  them,  from  starvation. 

Indian  Hostilities.— A short  time  since  anArkan- 
sas  paper  chronicled  the  fact  that  some  Brighamite  el- 
ders were  among  the  Cherokee  Indians  inciting -them  * 
‘against'  the  U. lS.1  The  latest,  accounts  are'  that 
they  show  a hostile  disposition.  A brother  Jwho  sita 
beside  us  wasTpresent  in  secret  council,  in  Nauvoo- 
some,  time  since,  when  the  .Chamankees  were  spo; 
ken  of  as  allies;  a‘ delegation  sent  to  visit  them,  and 
.measures  taken  to  secure  their  fidelity.  All  this  may 
work-the-destruction  both-of-instigators  and—instru- 
ments,  but  it  will  bring  no  good  to  any  body. 

[CTJ.  B.  Backenstos,  eheriffof  Hancock  county  Ills, 
has  been  appointed  by  the  President  a captain  in  the 
t U.  S.  Army.  A suitable  reward  for  the  zeal,,  ener- 
gy, and  courage  exhibited  by  him  in  suppressing  the 
mobs  in  that  county  last  year. 

Pusillanimous  Outrage. — Some  of  the  Brigham  - 
ites  in  Nouvoo  recently  went. out  in  disguise  a few 
miles  from  the  city  and  whiped  a Mormon  (Strang- 
le ) nearly  to  death.  Immediately  the  Brighamite 
paper  in  Nauvoo  cries  out  against  the  Anti -Mormons 
for  commencing  hostilities;  arid  their  rowdies  boast 
that  the  Antics  know  no  differci.ee  between  the  par-

A DREAM. 

I dreamed  a dream  but  dont  mistake, 
l often  dream  things  _widc_awako;_ 

But  if  this  dream  should  prove  a lie, 

You  have  it  just  as  cheap  a 1; 
l thought  1 saw  before  my  eyes, 

A form  of  goodly  shape  arise; 

I stood  a while  the  form  to  scan 

Aud  thought  it  looked  much  like  a man; 

All  who  ee’rsaw  a body  know 
What  all  the  members  have  to  do, 

And  if  the  parts  their  functions  change. 

The  body  soon  becomes  deranged; 

This  body  moved  with  skill  and  art 
W hile  all  the  members  played  their  part; 


but  as  I stood  this  form  10  view 
Though  strange,  tis  no  more  strange  that  true  accept  or  to  refuse  affraid; 


And  thus  with  craft  it  did  proceed 
The  trusting  members  to  mislead, 

But  when  itjhought  its  plans  mature 
And  counted  that  success  was  sure, 

Tiius  boldly  unto  fqem  he  said: 

My  brethren  I was  next  the  head. 

While  vet  with  us  the  head  remained: 
But  now  tis  gone  the  rightTclaim 
To  all  the  priviliges  and  powers, 

Which  it  possesses  in  prosperous  hours, 
And  as  beneath  me  you  all  stand 
I claim  the  right  you  to  command, 

You  as  inferiors  must  obey 
Whatever  I to  you  shall  say; 

These  are  my  rights  and  this  my  claim, 
Will  you  accept  them  and  sustain 
My  mensuros  and  by  them  abide 
While  I as  head  the  body  guide. 

Thus  spake  the  breast  and  silently 
-With  patience  waited  the  reply — ' 

Meanwhile  the  members  sore  dismayed 


A hand  liftseen  cut  off  the  head, 

And  still  the  body  was  not  dead; 

With  wonder  Struck  at  things  so  strange, 
My  mind  was  Very  mtfch  deranged; 
but  soon  my  wild  arid  wandering  thoughts, 
back  to  me  came,  and  with  them  brought 
These  Sad  reflections-,  fears,  arid  doubts 
Which  r'eason  vainly  trfed  to  tout; 

Arid  thVis  unto  myself  Leaid 
This  btfdy  better  had  been  dead: 

Without  ahriad  it  will  become, 
Ungovernable  blirid  arid  dumb; 

.Nbrcan  it  hear  what  Othets  say, 

"Nor  its* feelings  unto  theVri  crinyey 
Nor -see  the  snares  that  foes  would  lay, 

“To  dead ‘Hs  wandering  steps  astray, 
While^thus  my  mind  had  listless  strayed* 
The  headless  members  idle  laid: 

They  durst  not  move  fearing  that  they 
By  one  false  step  should  run  astray. 

But  as  I looked  behold  tho  breast 
With  more  assurance  than  the  rest. 

And  more  ambition  than  the  whole 
Resolved  to  gain  supreme  control ; 

And  for  this  purpose  jt  did  call 
JJpori  the  members  one  and  all, 

. And  when  they  altogether  came 
To  lay  in  its  pretended' claim 


Some  ignorant  of  what  was  right. 

Yet  knowing  who  would  win  must  fight, 
And  who  would  must  needs  be  led 
Resolved  to  uphold  the  breast  os  head. 

Who  error  sows  must  reap  confusion; 
Ignorance  is  parent  of  delusion; 

And  when  too  Into,  the  membors  find 
Their  leader,  like  themselves  is  blind. 

In  natures  law  strict  order  reigns, 

And  violntion.brings  its  pains: 

Its  keenest  pains  those  members  felt, 

Vet  knew  not  whence  the  blow  was  dealt, 
Their  head  cut  off  their  best  life  blood 
‘Gush’d  forth  a warm  and  crimson  flood 
They  saiv  it  not,  ah!  how  could  they  I 
With  the  head  their  eyes  were  taken  away, 
And  yet  porir  dupes  they  thought  they  saw 
In  spite  of  natures  brokon  law. 

They  by  the  breast  were  cramed  with  lies* 
•And  still  they  thdught  the  breast  was  wise. 
But  some  (although  their  peace  they  held,) 
With  deep  shagnri  the  wreck  beheld; 

And  to  pfrriservri  their  life  and  fame 
Supported  this  unfourided  claim, 

Or,  silent  waited  until  ihey 
Could  boldly  advocate  the  way 
Which  riaturcs  God  had  foreordained, 

And  follow  link  by  link  the  chain 


Of  great  events  which  is  the  key 
To  unlocked,  hidden  mystery; 

. Arid  while  upon  this  scene  l look’d 
The  body  its  departure  took 
And  as  it  vanished  from  my  sight 
.■^'•'Tritp.  a vacuum  dark  as  night  . , 

A still  small  voice  whispered  to  me 

That  headless  body  you  no  more  shall  see.  ] 

The  fact  of  the  coming  forth  of  aucient 
American  records,  in  exact  fulfilment  of 
prophecies,  through  the  instrumentality  of 
Seers,  ought  to  gladden  every  mans  heart.  ‘ 
What  a glory  will  the  mysteries  of  .these  rec- 
ords by  and  by  shed  forth  upon  our  benighted 
world.  Some  years  since  I was  very  fortu- 
nate to  get  hold  of  an  old  -book  printed,  in 
Germanysome  hundreds  ot  years  ago,  con- 
taining many  curiousjiistorical  facts  which 
I considered  worthy*  of  notice..  Among  ma- 
riy  other  interesting  things,  it  said;  “As  ear- 
ly as  in  the  days  of  Enoch,  records  wore  en- 
graved upon  plcttesof  copper  or  brass.  Those 

plates-were  nanded  down  to  Noahwho  wrote 

on  them,  They  came  into  the  hands  of  Abra- 
ham, Isaac,  and  Jacob,  who  left  them  to  Jo- 
seph^.  Doubtless  these  records  must  con- 
tain many  mysteries  which  have  not  been 
revealed  to  the  world,  and  were  kept  secret 
among  those  who  were  in  possession  of  the 
holy  priesthood.  What  has  become  ot  these 
precious,  original,  patriarchal  records,  the 
book  of  Mormdnbseems  to  reveal,  arid  are 
they  not  the  very  plates  of  brass  that  Lehr 
brought  with  him  from  Jerusalem*  when  he 
emigrated  to  the  land  of  Joseph,  which  plates 
were  preserved  until  that  time  by  the  Elders 
of  that  tribe  after  Joseph’s  death,  and  con- 
taining nhe^revelatioirof  God  from  the  be- 
gining,  to  come  forth  in  the  latter  days,  inf 
order  te  be  united  with  the  record  or  Stik  of 
Judah.  A discerning  mind  has  no  trouble  to 
understand.  LEWIS  VAN  BUREN. 

Character  of  the  Men  Who  Dcjg  thk 
Plates  at.  Vorbe. 

Our  curiosity  was  sufficiently  exchetf,  to  in- 
duce us  to  make  ourself  more  fully  acquain- 
ted with  the  circumstances  and  facts.  For 
this  end  we  visited  the  gentlemen  alluded  to 
as  Seer  or  Prophet.  We  were  cordially  re- 
ceived, the  plates  were  shown  us,  and  we 
examined  the  spot  from  which  they  purport 
to  have  been  taken! 

The  Prophet  appear s to  us  a tcry  in'elli - 
gent  man  devoid  of  any  thing  like  enthusi- 
asm: and  so  far  as  We  could  judge  honest  and 
earnest  in  all  he  said.  The  men  who  sub- 
scribe the  statement  are  said  to  be  among  the 
most  honest  and  intelligent , in  the  neighbor- 
hood; and  take  it  all  together  it  is  something 
to  stagger  an  ordinary  credulity.  The  pop* 
tilar  opinion  will  doubtless  call  it  a humbug, 
so  should  we  from  the  natural  impulse  of  our 
mind,  but  when  the  testimony  appears  in  op- 
position to  such  impulse,  u e d~c  content  to 
have  no,  opinion  about  it . [Squpt  Tel.

new  city: 

“Voree  is  on  the  line  of  Racine  and  H al- 
worth  counties,  Wisconsin,  at  the  spot  where 
White  river  crosses  the  line.  Tho  main 
road  from  both  Racine  and  Southport,  to 
Elkhorn,  Janesville,  etc.,  to  the  Mississippi, 
and  also  to  Beloit  and  Galena,  passes  White 
river  here,  it  being  the  best  and  most  travel- 
ed road  in  the  Territory.  The  town  plat  is 
on  the  south  end  of  Gardner’s  Prairie,  which 
is  about  one  and  a halt  miles  wide,  and  three 
miles  long,  and  is  from  six  io  twenty  feet  a- 
bove  the  river,  gently  rolling,  a very  dry 
lime  gravel  soil.  Several  largo  springs  of 
clear  water,  perlectly  unchanging  both  in 
volume  and  temperature,  emerge  from  tho 
banks  a few  rods  from  the  river.  White 
river  furnishes  the  most  desirable  water  pow- 
er in  the  territory — twenty-five  feet  fall  (by 
means  of  a canal)  on  a stream  the  extrenio 
rise  and  fall  of  which  does  not  exceed  eigh- 
teen inches,  and  constantly  affording  power 
for  fifty  run  of  stones.  For  mechanical  pur- 
poses another  power  can  bo  had,  on  tho 
ryr>A  stream,  two  miles  above.  One  is  al- 
ready occupied  at  the  beautiful  and  flourish- 
ing village  of  Burlington,  one  mile  below. 
Honey  Creek  and  Sugar  Creek,  coming 
down  on  the  north  end  of  the  prairie,  giving 
additional  facilities  for  manufactories.  The 
prairie  is  sum  unded  by  gentle  hills  from 
sixty  to  two  hundred  feet  high,  covered  with 
timber,  thus  securing  Voree  and  the  whole 
prairie  from  the  winds  so  unfavorable  in  prai- 
rie countries,  and  giving  a beaiHiful  and  ro- 
mantic appearance  to  the  place.  The  im- 
provement of  the  water  power

ed the  reqtiium  of  martyred  prophets.

wolves  are  on  the  scent  die,"  Joseph  bid  lus 
wife  nnd  mother  farewell  saying,  I am  going 
as  a lamb  to  the  slaughter;  this  was  Ins  im-

\( Mvoo,  March  1st . 1846. 

For  ih<  Yorrt  UtfUi 

To  the  Saints  scatered  abroad  in  all  the 
W orld,  Greeting: 

I feel  it  a dntv  that  I owe  to  G**J,  the 
Church,  and  the  age  in  which  I live,  to  make 
known  to  >ou,  some  Facts  in  relation  to  the 
j r.*M:nt  situation  of  the  Church  of  Jesus  Christ 
ot  Latter  day  Saint*;  first  then  be  it  known 
unto  all  tnen  that  Jamet  J.  Strang  is  the 
Presidon*.,  Prophet,  Seer,  and  Revelater,  to 
this  church  appointed  by  Joseph  Smith,  ac- 
cording io  the  Book  of  Doctrine  and  Cove- 
nant*, before  hi*  martyrdom  ami  confirmed 
bv  the  ministry  or  Angola,  according  to  the 
order  of  <ucli  o>Jling*  and  priesthood.  The 
question  may  be  Aed,1  how  do  you  know 
that  such  is  the  fact;  i answer  that  I know 
by  the  same  testimony  tint  1 received  con- 
cerning brother  Joseph;  1 mean,  testimony 
from  God  by  revelation.  1 know  he  is  the 
only  man  that  has  claimed  that  calling  ac- 
cording to  the  order  of  the  Church  and  Book 
of  Doctrine  and  Covenants.  I know  he  is 
the  only  man  that  is  now  giving  proof  to  the 
Nation*  of  the  Earth,  p 4 such  Calling’*, 
Priesthood,  and  Authority.  ' % 

My  Moved  br^br  m m all  ,kn«w  th \t 


previous  to  the  death  of  brother  Joseph,  I 
was  acting  as  his  counselor  and  spokesman; 
you  also  know  that  us  soon  as  the ; Twelve 
usurped  authority,  J withdrew  from  their  un- 
holy, and  pernicious  councils — after  which 
they  attempted,  in  their  illegal  and  usurped 
authority,  to  cast  me  ofT  and  blacken  my 
character,  and  injure  my  usefulness.  But 
you,  amon^  whom  they  and  myself  have  trav^ 
eled,  can  judge  of  their  conduct  and  mine, 
and  you  know  the  “errors”  that  I commit- 
ted were  by  their  teachings  and  direct  com- 
mands, which  “errors”  i have  forever  re- 
nounced and  forsaken,  God  being  my  help- 
er. If  they  had  done  the  same,  God  would 
not  have  forsaken  them;  “but  they  love  dark- 
ness rather  than  light,  because  their  deeds 
are  evil.”  After  1 withdrew  from  them  and 
their  wicked  ways,  1 determined  never  to 
take  a stand  until  1 could  take  one  in  right- 
eousness and  truth,  according  to  the  order  of 
the  House  of  God,.  The  time  has  come  und 
the  man  also.  After  calling  on  God  in  the 
name  of  his  Son  Jesus,  he  condescended  in  a 
glorious  manner,  to  manifest  his  will  and 
purpose  to  me,  concerning  brother  James  J. 
Strang;  and  I now  bear  testimony  to  all  tho 
world,  that  he  is  a Prophet,  Seer,  And  lleve- 
lator,  appointed  and  chosen  of  God,  to  stand 
in  the  place  of  brother  Joseph;  to  give  the 
word  of  God  and  hold  the  keys  and  power 
that  is  to  bear  off  this  last  dispensation. 

GEORGE  J.  ADAMS. 

Jjcicislurgh , Ohio,  July  6/A,  1846. 


TIIJB  CITY  OF  VOREE. 

All  hail!  ye  saints  both  far  and  near, 

My  muse  invites  you  all  fu  hoar 
Glad  tidings  of  great  jo  v and  peace, 
They'll  make  your  doubts  and  darkness 
cease. 

The  dark  foreboding  clouds  obscure 
The  light  that  once  was  cleur  und  sure, 
Yet  there’s  a light  you  now  may  see 
In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

There  is  a land  the  Lord  hath  blessed, 

A land  of  peace — a land  'of 
He's  made  a secure  abode, — - 
To  all  that  love  the  word  of  God, 

A place  of  knowledge  and  of  light*. 

Of  wisdom,  glory,  strength  and  migT»T, 

Ho*  all  ye  saints  then  como  and  see, 

It  lathi© City  of  Voree. 

This  pleasant  land  abundance  yields. 

And  with  rich  blessings  crowns  tho  fields, 

'Tis  aureiy  a luxuries  soil 

And  well  repays  the  loborers  toil: 

AH  needful  blessings  God  bestows, 

To  make  the  place  a swoet  repose, 

That  &ainu  from  suffering  might  be  free, 
In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

The  angele  too,  ha\'e  blessed  the  place, 
With  messages  of  truth  and  grace 
Sent  forth  from  shining  worlds  above, 

To  ahow  God’s  wisdom,  power,  and  love. 
'Thus  truth  springs  out  from  under  ground, 
To  testify  to  all  around, 

That  James,  ©.prophet's  called  to  be, 

And  lead  God's  Church  in  fair  Voree. 

God  has  raised  up  a Prophet  there, 
liis  last-day  Kingdom  to  prepare. 

And  make  his  Wi U and  purpose  known, 
T“  a//  that  ai*// hns  PrAohe!  Wn: 


Thus  hidden  things  are  brought  to  light. 
And  Earth  and  Heaven  their  power  unite, 
To  bring  a blessed  jubilee, 

In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

An  ensign  God  has  raised  up, 

It  waves  upon  the  mountain’s  top — 

The  standard  of  the  King  of  Peace, 

To  gather  Israel’s  scattered  .race. 

Then  hasie  ye  heralds,  bear  the  news, 

To  gentile  nations,  and  the  Jews; 

Let  Israel’s  remnants  gathered  be 
In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

There  doth  the  escaped  of  Israel  dwell, 
Where  ancient  saints  in  battle  fell, 

Who  bv  transgression  fell  a prey 
To  wicked  men  in  bloody  fray. 

Then  let  the  saints  who  now  are  blessed 
With  that  delightsome  place  of  rest, 

Keep  God’s  commands  and  faithful  be, 

In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

God,  did  his  servant  Joseph  call, 

To  make  his  mercy  know  to  all, 

His  last-day  purposes  reveal 
And  all  the  tribes  of  Israel  seal — 

But  wicked  men,  in  bloody  strife, 

Have  sought  and  taken  his  sweet  life; 

But  now  his  place  is  filled  you  see, 

By  James  J.  Strang,  of  fair  Voree. 

Second 

God  hath  renewed  his  work  again, 

To  cleanse  his  people  from  their  sin, 

By  which  they  have  defiled  his  cause, 

Ana  set  at  naught  his  holy  laws; 

Then  purge  yourselves,  ye  saints  from  sin, 
That  you  in  peace  may  enter  in, 

And  with  rich  blessings  favored  bo, 

In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

Hark!  whet  sad- tidings — ah!  and  true, 

We  hear  from  beautiful  Nauvoo, 
ljow  fallen,  Oh!  how  fallen  are, 

Some  chosen  ones,  through  Saluns  snare. 
Frail  man,  how  weak — how  prone  to  sin, 
How  easy  let  the  tempter  in 
With  all  his  snares  of  lust  and  pride 
When  they  in  Christ  do  not  abide. 

In  ancient  days  we  find  that  man 
Did  oft  prevent  God's  holy  plan, 

Corrupt  the  Gospel  of  his  Son, 

And  fall  from  grace  as  they  have  done. 
Then  let  the  saints  take  hoed  lest  they-— 
By  Satan's  wiles  are  led  astray, 

Sir.ee  through  God’s  merev,  lie 
Will  gi\  e us  peace  in  fair  Voree.

Yet  stained  his  name  with  deep  disgrace; 
For  he  had  seven  hundred  wives,

Are  those  who  God's  true  glory  seek.

Be  all  your  boast— be  all  your  pride,  'f- 
Direct  your  wayathat  you  may  be 
A holy  people  in  Voree. 

No  black-leg  gentry  need  to  come, 

Intent  to  make  Voree  their  home, 

I o bring  on  saints  a deep  disgrace, 

And  stain  with  infamy  the  place. 

They’ll  find  no  genial  spires  there, 

The  Shepherd  guards  his  flock~w!th  care; 
All  such  will  be  advised  to  flee 
From  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

'Guinst  speculation  we  declaim, 

'Tis  Satans  legalized  game — 

A gentile  plot — to  mischief  prone, 

And  saints  should  let  this  game  alone: 

Let  brethren  help  each  other  there, 

That  God  may  hear  their  honest  prayer, 
And  send  them  blessings  full  und  free, 

In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

We’ll  not  invite  those  saints  to  come,  * 

Who  love  two  Gods  instead  of  one, 

For  by  Christ's  teaching  you  may  $ee. 

That  God  and  mammbn  dont  agree, 

And  those  who  love  God  by  protest, 

And  love  the  world  a little  best, 

Will  find  we  hope  small  company, 

In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

To  Subbath-break  ersAve- will  sav, 

Do  not  profane  God's  holy  day; 

It  is  a day  the  Lord  Jiatii  blessed, 

A day  of  peace  and'Sacred  rest; 

Then  call  the  Sabbath  a delight,  » 

And  in  God's  worship  ^llamite, 

That  you  may  dwell  there,  long  and  free, 
In  the  fair  City  of  Voree. 

We’ll  not  invite  our  breehren  there, 

Who  would  not  of  such  things  bewarej 
And  call  on  Israel’s  God  to  aid, 

That  Satan's  hellish  power  be  staid; 

For  surely  Jie  comes  down  in  power, 
Among  the  saints— in  this. sad  hour, 

In  fair  Nauvoo — but  let  them  flee, 

They  will  be  safe  in  fair  Voree. 

Then  let  the  heralds  loud  proclaim 
These  tidings,  in  the  Saviours  name, 

\ cu,  let  the  messengers  of  pence, 

Proclaim  old  Israels  full  release;

Being  clothed  upon  With  light  divine. 

Her  converts  come  and  be  made  free,

From  Satans  power — that  we  may  be 
A holy  people  in  Voree. 

Now  let  the  saints  with  heart  and  voice, 

In  these  glad  tidings,  all  rejoice. 

For  darkness  brooded  o’er  God’s  cause, 
While  chosen  men  transgressed  his  laws; 
But  now  the  light  doth  shine  so  clear, 

We’ll  cast  uway  all  doubt  and  fear; 

Forsake  our  sins,  and  gathered  be, 

In  tho  fair  City  of  Voree.

Sandy  Foundation. — Brother  Hyde  is 
very  much  troubled  lest  -some  body  shall 
build  themselves  up  on  the  faults  of  the 
Twelve.  Dont  be  uneasey  brother  Hyde, 
We  dont  build  on  sand,  though  the  bank  is 
as  broad  as  the  ATLANTIC. 

Brother  Hyde  travels  over  the  country  to 
warn  the  Saints  to  beware  of  certain  Elders 
whom  he  has  corrupted  and  requires  them 
to  yield  obedience  to  him,  while  he  yet  re- 
mains in  his  coiruptions,  lest  following  after 
the  teachings  of  those  who  have  repented  of 
the  evils  he  yet  abides  in,  they  shall  be  cor- 
rupted by  them. 

Going  Out  of  thb  Nation.— In  Oct. 
1845, Brigham  Young  and  his  fellow  usurpers 
put  forth  a proclamation,  calling*  on  all  the 
Saints  to  go  out  of  this  Nation,  and  hide 
themselves  up  in  the  Wilderness,  beyond  the 
Rocky  Mountains  till  the  indignation  of  God 
was  past.  They  were  required  to  go  out  of 
the  Nation  because  of  its  intolerant  and 
persecuting  spirit,  to  a country  where  they 
could  be  governed  by  the  laws  of  God,  and 
the  principles  of  true  Freedom.  (See  their 
public  proclamation.) 

In  addition  to  this;  Elders  were  sent 
privately  to  the  branches  to  tell  them  that, 
the  Indians  would  be  sont  down  on  the 
Frontiers,  led  by  Warlike  Elders  to  destroy 
the  country,  and  execute  vengeance  on  the 
people  ; and  unless  they  went  with  the  Church 
they  would  fall  with  the  Gentiles  in  the 
sweeping  desolation.  They  have  gone  pct-^ 
haps  one  tenth  the  distance,  and  behold  500 
of  them  by  counsel  of  Brigham  arc  enlisted 
into  the  U.  S.  service,  to  go  and  conquer 
Calilornia,  the  very  place  of  their  destination 
and  add  it  to  the  U.  S,  .Going  to  fight  the 
battles  of  the  Nation  from- which  they  are 
exiled  by  violence  ; erilarge  the  boundaries 
of  a country  which  they  say  is  too  persecu- 
ting for  the  Saints  to  lice  in  ; and  fleeing 
from  the  power  of  those  who  killed  the  Pro- 
phet, and  exiled  them  from  their  homes,  arc 
ready  to  spill  their  own  blood  in  extending  its 
dominion  over  the  very  country  in  which 
they  are  seeking  refuge.  Oh  shame ! where 
is  thy  blush. 

Consistency. — Hyde  and  Taylor,  are 
very  fearful  lest  the  Saints  shall  go  astray, 
consequently  all  the  Saints  are  warned  not 
to  reject  the  Twelve  in  consequence  of  their 

iniquities,  but  to  receive  them  without  any 
evidence  of  their  authority,  because  iniquity 
does  not  prove  that  they  have  no  Priest- 
hood. * y

Letter  of  Lucy  Smith  Mother  ia  Israel 
to  the  Brighamite  Trustees,  in  answer  to  a 
proposition  from  them  that  she  could  have 
her  inheritance  only  on  condition  that  her 
son  William  should  not  be  admitted  to  her 
house.  % i 

March22dt  1846. 

Messrs  Babbitt,  Hey  wood  ana  Fulmer. — I 
received  your  letter  of  to-day,  by  the  hand 
of  the  black  boy,  and  I may  inform  you  that 
I cannot  describe  my  feelings  when  I peru- 
sed its  contents,  such  proscribed  views  as 
you  have  there  advanced  shows  plainly  that 
I am  the  agrieved  party,  wronged  as  l am 
out  of  a home,  long  promised  to  me  by  my 
son,  and  since  his  death  the  promises  were 
renewed  all  last  Summer  and  Winter,  and 
the  last  thing  that  Brigham  said  to  me  was, 
l should  have  a home  and  be  provided  for, 
in  all  my  wants,  and  I think  now  if  he  were 
here  he  would  not  do  as  you  have  done,  but 
you  restrict  my  conscience,  put  limits  to  my 
affections,  threaten  me  with  poverty,  if  I do 
not  drive  my  children  from  my  door  because 
they  resent  insult  and  abuse,  that  has 
been  heaped  upon  them  without  measure, 
but  I grieve  for  them,  I am  old  my  feelings 
arc  tender ! Yet  l must  not  complain.  No, 
although  my  children  have  been  the  Fathers 
and  Founders  of  the  Church,  and  spent  their 
all  in  its  service,  yea  have  not  withheld  their 
lives,  but  have  been  sacrificed  on  the  alter  of 
Mobocracy  and  at  the  feet  of  wicked  men, 
have  boon  torn  from  their  widowed  Mother. 
This  is  not  enough  but  I am  called  upon  to 
banish  .from  my  home  the  few  of  my  family 
who  are  left  as  my  only  solace,  ao  you  so 
proudly  and  wickedly  %ask  me  to  do,  or  mv 
support  shall  be  withheld  from  me,  but  thank 
kind  Heaven  that  has  implanted  in  my  bosom 
affection  which  gold  cannot  buy,  nnd  which 
bribes  cannot  break  the  cords  of  affection 
that  binds  me  to  the  children  of  my  bosom 
even  eternity  itself  cannot  break,  they  are 
interwoven  with  the  finest  aiterieo  of  my 
heart,  and  the  love  that  flows  through,  them  ia 
the  only  principle  that  enlivens  and  cheers 
me  in  this  vale  of  tears.  You  would  have 
me  forsake  my  children  in  order  that  you 
may  give  me  a living,  but  let  it  not  he  said 
that  in  the  Church  of  Jesus  Christ  of  Latter 
Day  Saints,  a mother  has  to  forfet  all  natures 
ties,  to  cut  asunder  the  cord9  of  affection 
that  bind  her  to  her  children,  or  she  shall 
not  have  a subsistence.  Tell  it  not  to  the 
World,  let  it  not  bo  heard  among  natures 
sons  of  tho  forest.  I think  no  Christian 
Spirit  could  have  dictated  such  cold  charity 


to  me  or  aoy  one  who  merits  other  treatment- 
A headless  body  has  no  life  you  say,  but  it 
may  have  pockets  into  which  the  head  when 
in  its  place  put  the  means  of  my  subsistence. 
I do  look  for  help  somo  way.  Provide  me 
a house  and  do  as  you  think  best,  if  I suffer 
more  my  God  will  plead  my  cause,  some- 
thing mu3t  be  done  for  Spring  is  coming  da. 
As  to  the  head  of  the  Church  I am  Mother 
and  ask  obedience  to  the  Law  of  God,  and 
all  will  be  right  and  hone  that  feel  os  Joseph 
did  will  wroog_his  Mother,  his  Brother,  or 
his  Sisters.  What  is  done  L.  would  like  to 
have  done  immediately.  Give  mo  a deed  to 
a house  and  lot  and  advance  the  Quarterly 
sum.  A part  of  my  family  have  left  mo  to- 
day and  I expect  William  to  begone  soon 
and  he  will  go  and  como  as  the  Lord  directs 
him.  I wish  to  be  cultivating  n garden  soon, 
I have  no  means,  no  food  but  coarso  corn 
meal  and  I am  old  and  feeble  in  health. — 
Will  you  call  and  sec  me  and  talk  on  these 
affairs* » As  to  William  tie  is  my  aon  and 
he  has  rights.  As  to  dhe  twelve  you~say 
they  have  rights,  but  who  .shall  decide  be- 
tween them.  Are  you  the  judgo.  The 
Twelve  speak  against  William,  and  William 
speaks  in  his  own  defence.  You  say  ho 
slanders  them,  he  says  they  have  sladdorcd 
him  and  robbed  him  of  his  rights  and  done 
other  things  as  well  but  l shall  leavo  these 
things  to  one  who  is  a just  God  and  will 
measure  to  all  men  their  just  deserts  in  tho 
day  of  accounts.  As  to  the  merits  of  my 
children  none  are  more  worthy  to  fiavo  an 
inheritance  in  the  city  of  Joseph  and  you  aro 
now  living  on  tho  labor  of  their  hands.  I 
will  not  speak  of  this  further,  as  it  excites 
ray  tender  feelings,  to  think  that  any  should 
be  so  heartless  as  to  consider  that  after  their 
labors  for  years  in  sickness, in  persecution, 
and  perils  by  sea  and  land,  aud  suffering  pri- 
vations and  the  loss  of  all  thing  to  protect 
tbo  Prophet  my  son  and  build  up  tho  King- 
dom of  God,  and  now  they  are  not  worthy 
of  an  inheritance.  O shame  ! where  ia  thy 
blush  ? Let  this  bo  a sufficient  rebuke  from 
your  Mother  in  Israel,  Amen. 

Lucy  Smith  Mother  in  Israel.

God  forbid.

President  Strang  Derfr  Brother.  I haste 
to  inform  you  that  I have  Just ’visited  this 
place  for  the  purpose  of  calling  the  Saints 
together,  and  if  possible  organising  them  in  * 
the  truo  order  of  the  Church,  and  under  the 
true^  Presidency  and  succession  of  Priest- 
hood according  to  the  appointment  of  God. 

I found  them  in  an  unhappy  disorganised 
state,  verily  ‘ believing  that  the  Gospel  had 
been  taken  from  this /Nation.  They  did  not 
even  dare,  to  meet  together  for  the  admini- 
stration of  the  emblems  of  the  Broken  Body, 
and  Shed  Blood,  of  our  Lord  Jesus  Christ. 
After  addressing  them  three  or  four  times, 
Shewing  them  the  true  order  of  the  Church,- 
they  all  came  forward  like  Saints  of  God 
should  come,  and  voted  to  sustain  the  Au- 
thorities that  have  been  appointed  by  Reve- 
lation. They  are  17  in  number. 

Elder  Delong,  was  ordained  to  the  office 
of  a Highpriest,  and  appointed  to  preside 
and  they  are  now  rejoicing  in  hope  of  the 
Glory  of  God.  Union  and  Pence  prevail  in 
their  midst?  To  God  be  all  the  glory  ameu. 
as  over  I remain  yours  truly, 

G.  J.  ADAMS.

The  unconditional  Promise  made  to  the

In  all  the  covenants  made  by  God  with

which  is  yet  to  be  made  we  find  no  condi- 
tion^. ’ 

It  has  however  the  following  peculiari- 
ties : 

When  it  is ‘made  we  find, 

1st— That  it  will  bo  made  only  with  the 
Jews.-  

2nd — That  his  people  the  Jews  will  be 
without  sin. 

3rd — God  will  write  his  laws  in  their 
hearts. 

dth — They  shall  all  know  God  from  the 
least  to  the  greatest. 

5th — There  will  be  no  more  teachers.  * 

6th — -The  Heathen  shall  know  that  the 
Lord  has  sanctified  Israel. 

7th — The  Sanctuary  of  God  will  be  in  the 
midst  of  them  forever. 

8th — The  Jews  shall  dwell  in  the  land  of 
Palestine  for  ever/ 

9th — David  will  be  their  king  forever.
